COLLISION RECORDS, ARITHMETIC, AND RAW LOCAL INVERSION IN
               A TRIANGULAR LORENTZ GAS

                                                    QIAN QI


        Abstract. For the original triangular Lorentz gas with disk radii in [9/20, 47/100], we prove a
        complete physical-roof source-height estimate on the class with a fixed margin in its original
        incidence and all-obstacle clearance tests. A finite grid of endpoint momenta parametrizes genuine
        families of trajectories; each physical point belongs to at most 25 such families, independently of
        collision count. A two-sided optical Jacobian estimate pays every contributing roof coefficient
        by a positive volume of its entire physical family. This yields uniform roof-height bounds for
        critical and noncritical source, without a whole-cylinder pressure premise. Combined with
        the retained exponential confinement of every global disk-word minimum, it proves a new
        collision-normalized positive-source payment for deep clearance, incidence and exact-section
        obstructions. The central comparison keeps every collision, all original marks, the true physical
        clock and its signed endpoint work. We also control the actual inverse-gradient volume at
        critical roofs and prove an explicit guarded physical-word pressure estimate. A separate native
        calculation completes the stable-arclength optical comparison with uniform constants 30000 and
        6400. All earlier mathematical bodies and execution evidence are retained. The unrestricted
        pressure premise, uncovered boundary source, shallow comparison capacity and full spectral and
        conditional interfaces remain explicit requirements of the original raw pointwise local limit and
        four-coordinate inversion; those final theorems are not claimed complete.




                                               Introduction
   A physical collision record carries more information than the displacement of a particle at a
large time. In the triangular Lorentz gas considered here, the observations are the two integer
lattice coordinates, the total unit-speed physical time, and the integer occupation of a specified
collision section. Conditioning on the original initial and terminal section tests identifies the
actual return event. The problem is to obtain its raw pointwise local limit, with all collision
labels and microscopic roof windows retained, and then the four-coordinate local inverse theorem.
   The fixed family consists of equal circular scatterers on the triangular lattice, with R ∈
[9/20, 47/100]. The collision probability is dα dp/(4π) and its original section normalization is
c∗ = 91/(10000π). The event (1.1), arithmetic reference (1.4) and target (1.5) are unchanged. In
particular occupation counts the initial state and excludes the terminal state, whose membership
is tested separately.
            p         All reflections are actual first collisions. The physical flight bounds τ− = 3/50
and τ+ ≤ 3847/124 < 28/5 and the finite lattice budgets are proved in Section 2.
The new physical source-height mechanism. The present revision advances the positive-
source problem in its original pointwise roof topology. The earlier exponential total-variation
estimate alone could not do this: a small neighborhood of a critical point can contain an entire
small roof ellipse. We now compare each roof coefficient with a complete two-dimensional
family of actual nearby trajectories and pay the overlap of all such families before taking a roof
supremum.

   The constants in (0.1) are conservative. Their mathematical significance is that they are fixed

unit directions, active index set and active vertices. Their possible differences lie along inactive
straight chains. No uniqueness of the full minimum, physical continuation to it, or billiard flow
through a singular reference is asserted.

Clearance of all actual obstacles and an exact source. A change parallel to an original
flight should be accounted for using its two actual incident disks. Lemma 46.1 does this with
βi = max(|vi⊥ · δi |, |vi⊥ · δi+1 |):
                             Γi (Y ) ≥ min{Γi (Q), τ− } − βi         (2βi < τ− ).
Here Γi is the clearance of the complete closed segment from every third lattice disk, including a
disk visited elsewhere in the same word. The proof treats the strip projections of the incident
disks, the longitudinal extensions, endpoints and changes of the minimizing center. Its quadratic
transverse budget is the retained refinement of the fourth-power Hausdorff budget.
   Together with (0.1), the strip theorem provides exponentially small true clearance and incidence
witnesses for any deep physical failure of any minimum. A separate pointwise phase estimate
supplies the witness for disagreement with the exact original section. The original section’s collar
satisfies the explicit bound ν{dist∞ (x, ∂YR∗ ) ≤ ρ} ≤ ρ/(2π) for 0 < ρ ≤ 1/200.

   The unconditional comparison statements are positive inequalities of measures pushed forward
by Lm,R (Q), the original full physical roof. The inherited estimates in Theorem 49.1 and
Corollary 50.4 identify exactly the additional input for a roof density: Input 11.1, with the
specified physical stable arclength, optical coordinate factors and uniform word-pressure constant.
Under that input the small costs have bounded density on the whole roof line. For dm = ⌈θm⌉,
0 < θ < 1/2, the exponential costs satisfy an O(m2 e−λ0 m ) bound after collision normalization,
with λ0 = θ| log qa |/94. Their central clock Fourier error is uniformly small on |s| ≤ eλm for every
λ < λ0 , after its exact boundary phase, and uniformly in both lattice twists and the return twist.
   These inherited conditional density estimates concern small comparison costs on the exact
central class. Total variation of its exceptional source does not estimate that source’s roof
essential height. Neither statement by itself supplies all physical multipliers, a full unbounded
Fourier integral or roofwise capacity. Theorem 53.2 now removes the pressure dependence for
these costs on the full specified Gε , while Theorem 53.1 pays the exceptional source’s normalized

The retained architecture and the remaining limit. All fifty complete predecessor core
proof bodies 260–309, the model and the analytic interfaces are preserved byte for byte and
included exactly once in the current native manuscript. They contain the optical Jacobi and
endpoint trace completion, marked incidence pressure, persistent low-load shortage, one- and
multiple-mark physical clearance, cross-flight control, homogeneous growth, complete critical-
sector summation, radial coarea, endpoint jets and resolvents, physical tubes, bending, and the
global variational and summable sublevel theorems. In particular the physical curve-growth
hypothesis is discharged by the native chain 281–283, and the complete regular critical coefficient
and common physical-roof unscaled summations are inherited results. Their completion is not
presented as a new assumption or a new contribution.
   The preserved historical native assembly carries the complete earlier geometry, periodic orbit,
arithmetic, inducing, spectral, conditional-measure and inverse chain. It contains 390 exact
source files and is rebuilt separately with its original assembler. The archive also retains all 355
historical numbered source variants covering 1–303, the six exact later bodies 304–309, every
original predecessor file and all 90 retrieved A2-DYN reports. The proof ledger distinguishes a
directly proved finite geometric assertion, a consequence at an inherited input, and the global
analytic inputs still requiring specialist reconstruction.

         2. An explicit all-direction physical horizon and lattice budget
   We give a quantitative proof of finite horizon for the unchanged round scatterers. It is
independent of a simulated trajectory, a symbolic model, a pressure theorem and the compactness
argument retained in Section 24. It makes the finite geometric constants in the physical source
construction directly auditable.
Lemma 2.1 (A row rectangle for a √  primitive lattice direction). Let v be a primitive vector of the
triangular lattice, l = |v|, and h = 3/(2l). For every q ∈ R2 there is a lattice center z whose
components relative to q, along v/l and its unit normal, satisfy |x| ≤ l/2 and |y| ≤ h/2. Thus
for a line direction making angle δ with v,
                                                                                         p
                |(z − q) · e⊥ | ≤ h2 | cos δ| + 2l | sin δ|,        |(z − q) · e| ≤ 12       l2 + h2 .   (2.1)
Proof. Primitivity permits v to be completed
                                       √         to a lattice basis. The parallel lattice rows are
consequently spaced by the covolume 3/2 divided by l. Choose a nearest row to q, and then a
nearest lattice center along that row. Its two coordinates obey the stated bounds, regardless of
the longitudinal offset of the row. Projection and Cauchy–Schwarz give (2.1). All choices are in
the original Euclidean lattice; no linear map deforms the circular disks.                       □
Theorem 2.2 (A uniform, explicit physical free-flight bound). For every R ∈ [9/20, 47/100],
every straight segment avoiding the interiors of all radius-R lattice disks has length at most

                                              124     5
In particular the same bound holds for every genuine free flight and all its grazing limits. The
statement is simultaneous over all initial points and all directions; no exceptional direction is
discarded.

to 0 ≤ ϑ ≤ π/6. Put t = tan ϑ/ 3 ∈ [0, 1/3]. We use four primitive lattice vectors, with the

the four lengths its largest value is 31 + 3/124/2 = 3847/124/2. If a free segment were longer

grazing free segments; no collision derivative at grazing is used.                                  □
Corollary 2.3 (Explicit enclosing candidate counts, not word counts). Keep the original constants
R+ = 47/100, δ∗ = 3/200, the intrinsic τ+ of the retained source papers, and every original
event. Then
           Nf ≤ 150,       N◦ = N∗ ≤ 150,          Npair ≤ 162,     Jf = 2Nf + 1 ≤ 301.
Here Nf is the first-hit candidate count of (24.1), N◦ is the one-mark/cross-flight count of
(19.7), and Npair is the difference count in (18.1). In particular the angular first-hit partition in
Lemma 21.2 has at most 1201 intervals. These are upper bounds for the intrinsic counts, not
assertions that all candidate centers can occur or that the smallest physical horizon is 28/5.

Their squares are respectively 106929/2500, 1718721/40000 and 431649/10000. The first two lie in
[42, 43) and the last in [43, 44). A lattice point with integer coordinates (a, b) has squared length
Q(a, b) = a2 + ab + b2 , which is integral. The enclosing sets are therefore exactly 0 < Q ≤ 42
and 0 < Q ≤ 43.
   For completeness this finite count does not depend on a floating-point circle. The identity
Q = (a + b/2)2 + 3b2 /4 implies |b| ≤ 7 when Q ≤ 43, and by symmetry |a| ≤ 7. Direct solution
of the quadratic inequality gives the following inclusive integer ranges for b ≥ 0:
                                   b a for Q ≤ 42      # a for Q ≤ 43           #
                                   0    [−6, 6]        13   [−6, 6]             13
                                   1    [−6, 5]        12   [−7, 6]             14
                                   2    [−7, 5]        13   [−7, 5]             13
                                   3    [−7, 4]        12   [−7, 4]             12
                                   4    [−7, 3]        11   [−7, 3]             11
                                   5    [−7, 2]        10   [−7, 2]             10
                                   6    [−6, 0]        7    [−7, 1]             9
                                   7   [−5, −2]        4   [−6, −1]             6
Negative rows have the same cardinalities under (a, b) 7→ (−a, −b). The totals, excluding the
origin, are 12+2(12+13+12+11+10+7+4) = 150 and 12+2(14+13+12+11+10+9+6) = 162.
The intrinsic candidate sets are subsets of these enclosing sets, which proves all three inequalities.
The stated branch bounds now follow from the already proved formulas 2Nf + 1 and 8N∗ + 1. No
collision word, pairing sheet, homogeneous grazing strip or probability normalization is counted
by these finite numbers.                                                                           □
   This quantitative result replaces no dynamical hypothesis by a finite simulation. It supplies
the geometric finite-horizon input used in the retained source and curve-growth proofs. It
does not supply the long-time marked pressure, the sum at a common physical roof, or the
collision-normalized positive-source estimate.

   At an endpoint of a fixed-θ segment the complete finite trajectory also has a limit. Indeed
Lemma 5.1 gives the initial angle and p0 = −z cos θ/R. For each designated next disk, the entry
root of the flight quadratic is continuous up to a tangency. The flight length cannot collapse to
zero, and the reflection v 7→ v − 2(v · n)n is continuous at tangency. Induction over the fixed
finite itinerary gives the limits of all visited points, velocities, τi and ci . The third-disk clearances
have limits as well: the finite horizon restricts the possible nearby disk centres to a finite set,
and the minimum distance to each of them is continuous. This argument takes only one-sided
limits from the actual event; it asserts no continuation through its boundary.

original regular pairing segment the coarea factor and weighted density are

                                                                    Z b

                 |f ε (v) − f ε (u)| ≤ C(1 + ε−1 )ϱ(m−2)+ |v − u|1/2         (a ≤ u < v ≤ b).   (5.6)
If a limiting trajectory at either endpoint contains an actually visited interior grazing collision,
its trace at that endpoint is zero. The original paired minimum on that entire segment is then
zero, for every original selector.
Proof. The determinant identity in (5.4) follows by expressing D arg g as e⊥T G/z. The preceding
endpoint-limit argument and Lemma 5.2 give limits for G, ω and χ, the last with a positive lower
bound. Every factor in the finite product defining W ε is continuous in its incidence or clearance
argument. Thus f ε has the asserted finite limits, including at a critical incoming endpoint, where
the direction θ is fixed.
   Corollary 4.4 and (5.4) bound AIm by Cϱ(m−2)+ . The integral in (5.5) is finite by Lemma 5.1.
On compact regular subsegments the flight charts are smooth and the finite clearance minima are
locally Lipschitz, so f ε is locally absolutely continuous. Integrating the inherited regular-point
derivative inequality on these subsegments and then passing to the proved endpoint limits
establishes absolute continuity and (5.5). The same argument between u and v, using (5.2), gives
(5.6).
   Finally, if cj → 0 at a visited interior collision, the marked optical bound gives ω → 0, while
0 ≤ W ε ≤ 1 and χ ≥ λ◦ . Hence that endpoint trace is zero. The paired density is its nonnegative
minimum with the other trace.                                                                    □
Remark 5.4 (Two different tangency mechanisms). The last assertion concerns a collision in the
visited itinerary. A flight which merely becomes tangent to an unvisited third disk has zero
clearance but need not have any cj = 0. If all visited incidences remain positive, then (4.7)
has a positive limit. A zero third-disk clearance makes W ε = 1 at the limit; consequently the
trace can be positive. Such sheets are not removed. Neither are nongrazing critical entrances or
positive section-boundary traces. For a nongrazing critical entrance the exact directional limit is
ω∗ W∗ε /(4πc∗ e⊥T G∗ e⊥ ). No roof-independent value at the critical point is asserted.

                        6. The complete physical comparison space
     The comparison space is now specified without an unverified endpoint-trace exclusion:
                                             Θad = Θphys .                                      (6.1)
More explicitly, it consists of all positive-length maximal regular pairing segments of the original
exact event, with their original incident-component identities. Their endpoints may be critical,
visited-grazing, third-disk-tangent, or section-boundary limits. Theorem 5.3 supplies finite traces
and the endpoint variation inequality on this space. The underlying Borel pairing construction
and the averaged analytic estimates remain the inputs in Section 3. No singular trajectory is
continued past its physical boundary, and no new source supported at a zero-length segment is
introduced.

            7. The original microscopic shortage after grazing payment
  All allocations below are scalar allocations at the original roof; they are not couplings of
individual source and comparison sheets. For any original source U (t) define
                 Vr,K (t) = min{U (t), KPr (t)},      Rr,K (t) = (U (t) − KPr (t))+ .
The multiplier is βr,K = min{1, KPr /U } on U > 0 and zero on U = 0. Then U = Vr,K + Rr,K at
finite count and Vr,K is the original source multiplied by βr,K (t). When a restricted comparison
space is used, source outside it is still in U and may receive this same global fraction. It is not
therefore retained sheetwise with coefficient one.

   This theorem supplies a physical component of the required scarcity estimate, rather than
a new certificate class. It removes an entire visited-grazing source class exactly and pays the
loss of all its comparison capacity by a faster-decaying original optical volume. It does not yet
bound HM (Rng   B ) by a quantity tending to zero. In N every visited endpoint incidence is positive
at each fixed count, but its infimum may tend to zero with the count. Third-disk tangencies,
nongrazing critical entrances, section-boundary cuts, flat and short-reach segments, and the
original comparison shortages all remain with their full weight. A collision-uniform estimate
on that remaining source is still required for (1.5). Neither finite-count exhaustion nor the
per-itinerary exponential bound is used in its place.

          8. A positive payment from the variation on a physical segment
   The comparison pool of Section 6 is left unchanged. We first make a different use of the
completed endpoint estimate: it pays part of the original source, whether or not that source has
a comparison window. All statements in this section are made on the inherited one-copy physical
pairing space.
   Write Creg for a fixed constant in (3.3). For fixed ε > 0 define
                                    V ε (θ) = Var[a(θ),b(θ)] f ε ( · , θ),

Endpoint values in the variation are the one-sided physical traces, not values assigned on a

Lemma 8.1 (Measurability and the full variation bound). Both functions in (8.1) are Borel
and finite on every positive-length physical segment. Moreover,
                                         Z b
                                   ε
                                   V =         |∂u f ε | du ≤ Creg (1 + ε−1 )J .                (8.2)
                                          a
This is a bound for total variation, not merely for the difference of the two endpoint values.
Proof. Pull the closed segment back to [0, 1] by u = a + s(b − a), using the endpoint traces at
s = 0, 1. The interior evaluations are jointly Borel. At s = 0 the completed value is the limit of
evaluations at a + ℓ/j, j ≥ 2; at s = 1 it is the limit of evaluations at b − ℓ/j. The completed
endpoint values, and hence the function on the closed product, are jointly Borel. The resulting
function is continuous in s. Its variation is the supremum of the sums of absolute increments
over finite rational partitions containing 0 and 1. Continuity permits this countable family of
partitions, so its supremum is Borel. The integral defining J is Borel by the same change of
variables and nonnegative integration. Theorem 5.3 gives absolute continuity on the closed
segment. The variation of an absolutely continuous real function is the integral of the absolute
value of its derivative. Integrate (3.3); its right side is integrable by Corollary 4.4 and Lemma 5.1.
This proves the equality, inequality and finiteness asserted above.                                □

           9. Persistent guarded segments and the same pointwise target
   The positive decomposition of the preceding section has a geometric consequence on every
segment which retains source. It uses the original incidence and clearance guard, without adding
a distance to the section boundary.
Theorem 9.1 (Persistence of the guard on the remaining source). Let γ > 1. If cε,γ
                                                                               per (θ) > 0,
then cε > 0 and, at every point of the closed physical segment,
                               |f ε (u, θ) − cε (θ)| ≤ V ε (θ) < γ −1 cε (θ).                         (9.1)

coefficient is paid: cε,γ    ε     ε,γ
                      var = c and cper = 0.

Proof. The endpoint minimum is attained at at least one endpoint, where f ε = cε . Every
increment from that endpoint is bounded by the total variation. Positivity of the remaining
coefficient is precisely cε > γV ε , which proves (9.1). If any visited interior incidence vanished at
an endpoint, Theorem 5.3 would give cε = 0. Thus the segment belongs to N . Its optical factor
A is finite and positive on the closed segment: the exact formula ω = Rc0 cm /bm in (4.7) has
a finite positive denominator when all visited incidences are positive, while (5.4) gives χ ≥ λ◦ .
This also applies at a critical endpoint with its fixed Gauss direction. Since f ε = AW ε > 0, the
guard is positive there. If all inequalities in (9.2) failed, every factor defining H ε would equal
one, contradicting W ε = 1 − H ε > 0.
   For the converse, let u0 be a point where W ε = 0. The finite optical factor gives f ε (u0 ) = 0,
so the increment from an endpoint attaining cε gives V ε ≥ cε . Apply (8.3). This argument also
covers u0 = a or u0 = b, with the proved physical one-sided values.                                 □

is Borel. The source UB is supported on it; its coefficient is still the positive difference in (8.3),
not the full original coefficient. The sum (8.8) retains every original coefficient exactly. No
assumption that one fixed bad test covers the entire segment is made. A Borel witness at a
specified point can be chosen as the first successful test in the finite ordered list in (9.2); this
selection is not needed to prove the payment.
Remark 9.2 (Boundary mechanisms and what is retained). An unvisited third-disk tangency may
give W ε = 1 at an endpoint without forcing variation relative to the endpoint minimum. It is
therefore not declared paid merely on account of its name. The same applies to a nongrazing
critical entrance, an original section cut and a short-reach segment. Each receives precisely the
two coefficients in (8.3). When the guard vanishes elsewhere on that segment the entire coefficient
is paid by Theorem 9.1; otherwise a positive remaining coefficient is possible. At fixed collision
count the optical data on a surviving segment have positive minima, but no such minimum is
asserted uniformly in the collision count or the sheet.

original raw pointwise theorem, and not an averaged substitute, remains the organizing target.

                        10. Global uniqueness on a roof–angle fiber
   The estimates in this section concern actual finite trajectories, not a continuation through a
forbidden event. In particular the regular domain of a lifted itinerary need not be connected or
convex. This distinction is important when the original section and occupation tests divide that
domain into many components.
   Normalize the initial lifted scatterer to O0 = 0. Let Wm (R) denote the realizable lifted
itineraries of m flights. This set is finite by the finite-horizon bound and the lattice geometry.
Fix w = (O0 , . . . , Om ) ∈ Wm (R). For a physical trajectory write Qj = Oj + Rnj , let tj be the
counterclockwise rotation of nj , and put

configuration in question because successive scatterer disks are disjoint. Write β0 = βm = 1 and
βj = 2 for 0 < j < m.

Theorem 10.2 (Global roof–angle uniqueness). For a fixed lifted itinerary, radius and direction
e ∈ S 1 , the physical length is strictly increasing among regular trajectories whose endpoint
gradients are g = ze, z > 0, ordered by z. At a fixed z there is at most one such trajectory. In
particular
                                      Q 7−→ (F (Q), arg g(Q))                               (10.4)
is globally injective on the noncritical regular trajectories of that itinerary. This remains true
after any of the original exact event tests, and across all their disconnected regular components.
Proof. If gb = g, the left side of (10.3) is zero. All cj and cbj are strictly positive, so every bj = 0.
Thus all physical contacts, and then all flight directions, coincide.
  Now let gb = zbe, g = ze and zb > z > 0. The configurations cannot coincide. At least one bj is
therefore positive, and (10.3) gives
                                                     X
                                (zb − z)e · sE ≥ R       βj (cj + cbj )bj > 0.
                                                     j

Hence e · sE > 0. The right side of (10.1) is then strictly positive, proving S(Q)b > S(Q).
Equal roof and angle would force equal z, and the first part of the proof would force equality
of trajectories. Restricting an injective map to the original exact event cannot increase its
multiplicity.                                                                               □
Lemma 10.3 (A bounded roof range for each physical itinerary). For any two regular physical
trajectories with the same itinerary,
                          |S(Q) − S(Q)|

22                                            QIAN QI

Proof. With its endpoint contacts fixed, a regular physical trajectory minimizes S over the
product of the closed interior scatterer disks. Indeed, for a competitor Yj in the jth disk,
nj · (Yj − Qj ) ≤ 0; the convex support inequality and ∇j S = −2cj nj therefore give the minimizing
property. Use the interior contacts of Q    b as a competitor for the endpoints of Q. Changing
only the first and last endpoint changes the sum of flight lengths by at most the sum of their
displacements, by the triangle inequality. This proves one direction of (10.5); interchanging the
trajectories proves the other. The same triangle inequality covers m = 1.                        □
   This range estimate does not control how many original occupation labels occur in a word
domain. In particular it is not used to replace the exact occupation test by an unqualified interval
test.
10.1. The original physical sum at a target roof. For the original section tests, χ ≥ λ◦
by (5.4). On each regular component, the map (10.4) is a local diffeomorphism. Theorem 10.2
shows that all those components of a fixed itinerary have disjoint images, not merely that each
component has a nonsingular Jacobian. The equal-gradient argument also gives at most one
regular critical trajectory per word, even when the gradient is zero. There are finitely many
physical words at each finite count, so these critical points have zero collision area. Consequently,
for any nonnegative Borel trajectory weight a, the original roof density has the representation
                  dF# (a1Π νR∗)       Z 2π   X                         ω(xw )a(xw )

Proposition 10.4 (Monotone completion of the target-roof formula). Formula (10.6) is an
identity of nonnegative measures for every nonnegative Borel weight, with extended values   allowed
before an integrability bound is proved. In particular it applies to the unbounded products i∈S c−1
                                                                                           Q
                                                                                                 i .

Proof. Fix m, R, n, k. Cover the noncritical regular physical domain in endpoint coordinates by
countably many relatively compact local charts Vj on which (F, arg g) is a C 1 diffeomorphism
into an unwrapped angular chart. Such a cover exists by the nonvanishing Jacobian and second
countability. Subdivide as necessary so that bothSmaps have bounded derivatives on each smaller
chart. Disjointize the source charts by Bj = Vj \ k<j Vk . The sets Bj are Borel; no chart overlap
is counted twice. Angular representatives are also split at a fixed seam, so their range is [0, 2π).
   For a nonnegative Borel weight h put
                                    hN = min{h, N }1S          Bj .
                                                         j≤N

This sequence increases to h on the regular noncritical domain. Apply ordinary change of
variables on each of the first N charts, restricted to its Borel piece, and test against a nonnegative
Borel function of (t, ϑ). The local inverse Jacobian, multiplied by the original collision density,
is exactly ω/(4πc∗ χ). Local inverses are continuous and agree where they represent the same
physical point, so their Borel restrictions define the displayed xw without a selection ambiguity.
Global uniqueness prevents different regular components of one word from producing the same
target. Distinct words are summed, not identified.
   Monotone convergence on the source, followed by Tonelli and monotone convergence on the
target, now gives the identity for h as an extended nonnegative measure. Only after this passage
is an upper pressure bound used to establish finiteness and an L∞ density. This avoids assuming
the integrability of the unbounded marked weight in order to prove its own change of variables.
                         COLLISION RECORDS AND RAW LOCAL INVERSION                                 23

   The complement of the two-sided regular set has zero collision area. On the countably many
charts just chosen, absolute continuity of the coordinate changes and their local Lipschitz property
give zero target area for its image. The finitely many regular critical points also have zero source
area. Removing these sets therefore does not change the weighted source measure. Fubini yields
the claimed roof identity for almost every t. All null sets here are chosen at the fixed radius; no
exceptional set common to every R is asserted or needed. For the finitely many marked sets at
a fixed m, intersecting the corresponding full-measure roof sets provides a common version for
their finite positive expansions.                                                                 □

The target-roof pressure theorem proves a new physical weighted summation and the pointwise
removal in Theorem 12.2; it does not prove (12.12). In particular the all-incidence bound Cε
furnished by (12.4) with L = 1 is unscaled. At a fixed outer B it cannot be multiplied by
m2 and declared small. Nor does an inverse-incidence insertion estimate an inverse third-disk
clearance. The remaining source includes the original shallow incidence witnesses, unvisited
tangencies, critical entrances, section cuts and capacity deficiencies which are not paid by the
proved estimates. No change of the original theorem or of its arithmetic classes is made in stating
this boundary.

use its inherited geometric inputs, not a new conditional mixing or local-limit assumption.

Theorems 13.1–15.1 prove new finite-count concentration and positive payments in the required
normalized topology. They do not prove (15.8). Low-load shallow incidences, arbitrary-depth
clearance (including unvisited tangency), and capacity-deficient positive source remain in that
equation. The original physical section, guard, arithmetic reference and exact-label pointwise
assertion have not been weakened. The distinction between this remaining assertion and the
proved payments is part of the theorem statement, not an inference from software diagnostics or
an editorial change of topic.

     16. Clearance-to-roof coarea without a fixed-clearance density bound
  We keep the physical parameters and every event in Section 1. In this section u is the clearance
coordinate of Section B, not physical time. Physical time is denoted by t throughout. Fix
R, m, n, k, a flight 0 ≤ j < m, a sign σ, and an actual regular marked itinerary. On a local
angular lift put
                 Qj (ϑ, u) = (α0 , αm )(XR,j (ϑ, σ, u)),      S(ϑ, u) = F (Qj (ϑ, u)).
The point used as a mark is an unvisited near miss, not an additional reflection. Every derivative
below is taken on the same designated regular itinerary. In particular m, j and the physical roof
are not changed by this parametrization.
Theorem 16.1 (The long-record Jacobian and its critical Hessian). Let A = DQj , let g = ∇q F ,
and let G = Dq2 F . On every such chart,
                                        | det A| = (Rω)−1 ,                                  (16.1)
                         | det D(ϑ,u) (S, arg g)| = χ/(Rω)    (g ̸= 0),                      (16.2)
                                                              2

                                                    √
                                       √              det G
                                              2
                                         det D S =           .                           (16.4)
                                                      Rω
These are identities for each finite physical collision count, with all coordinate and section-
normalization factors displayed. In particular the Hessian term containing g cannot be dropped
away from a critical roof.
Proof. The collision map preserves dα dp at fixed R. The signed line map at collision j has
absolute Jacobian R, by (B.4). Composition with TRj therefore still has absolute Jacobian R with
respect to the initial collision coordinates. The map (α0 , p0 ) 7→ (α0 , αm ) has absolute Jacobian
ω −1 by the definition of ω. Dividing the two determinants gives (16.1). It uses the actual inverse
iterate and is independent of where the mark occurs. On a noncritical chart the determinant
of q 7→ (F, arg g) is χ; the chain rule gives (16.2). Twice differentiating F ◦ Qj gives (16.3),
component by component. At g = 0 its last term vanishes, and the retained positive-definite

optical Hessian and the invertibility of A give the assertion. Taking the determinant and using
(16.1) proves (16.4).
   As a normalization check, the line-coordinate density is (4πRc∗ )−1 . Dividing it by the
determinant in (16.2) gives exactly ω/(4πc∗ χ), the original target-roof/Gauss-angle density.
There is no additional factor R, R−1 or c−1∗ . All chart restrictions and exact labels remain on
the source side of this change of variables.                                                   □

Lemma 16.3 (A one-dimensional band estimate). Let f ∈ C ℓ (I) on an interval, with integer
ℓ ≥ 1 and |f (ℓ) | ≥ κ > 0. For h > 0,
                           |{x ∈ I : a < f (x) < a + h}| ≤ 2ℓ(h/κ)1/ℓ .                     (16.5)

Proof. First restrict the band to a compact subinterval and let its measure be L > 0. Its
cumulative Lebesgue measure is continuous and one-Lipschitz. The ℓ + 1 quantiles with successive
mass gaps L/ℓ can be chosen in its closed support; their successive coordinate gaps are at least
L/ℓ. Equivalently one can use interior approximate quantiles and pass to their limits. Write
them as x0 < · · · < xℓ . Continuity puts f (xi ) in [a, a + h]. The divided-difference mean-value
theorem, proved by subtracting the interpolating polynomial and applying Rolle’s theorem ℓ
times, gives some ξ ∈ (x0 , xℓ ) for which

16.1. One-copy summation and the remaining uniformity. On a countable collection
of admissible physical charts, first replace their source sets by disjoint Borel differences. The
preceding density formulae then add exactly. Their positive upper bounds add as well, with an
extended value permitted until a finite bound has been proved. Two impact signs and distinct
unvisited centres are retained with precisely the multiplicity in (E.2); no full-word cardinality
is inserted. Borel event cuts do not require a new chart merely because they disconnect the
selected set.
   In particular the per-clearance condition (E.3) is not needed on a chart controlled by Theo-
rem 16.5. An angular fold may have an unbounded conditional density and a bounded integrated
density at the same time. What is still required for the full clearance payment is a bound for
the complete, exact-label, positively weighted chart sum, in the original m2 L∞ (dt) topology.
The local constants in (16.6) or (16.7) have not been proved to provide that bound. Charts
with Su = 0, degenerate angular behaviour outside a verified finite type, and singular physical
boundaries are not discarded. Regular full critical roofs are treated next by a different exact
calculation.

17. Critical roofs, actual reversing orbits, and their exact density coefficient
   We now identify a dynamical invariant in the critical-roof density, without averaging the roof
or replacing the physical trajectories. Let x be a regular m-flight trajectory with endpoints in
the original section cap. Write qx = (α0 , αm ) and tx = F (qx ). All statements about derivatives
of the actual collision map below concern regular trajectories, not a formal continuation through
a tangent collision.
Theorem 17.1 (Critical half-paths are physical reversing periodic orbits). The endpoint roof
is critical precisely when p0 = pm = 0. Such a trajectory extends by the actual reflection law to
a regular 2m-collision periodic orbit, whose period need not be primitive. The extra m flights
traverse the original flights in reverse, with total roof 2tx and total lifted displacement zero. Let
I(α, p) = (α, −p) be collision time reversal, and put
                                                  m−1
                                    AIm,R (x) =         1YR∗ (ITRj x).
                                                  X

                                                  j=0
If the half-path belongs to the original exact event, then
                                 A2m,R (x) = Am,R (x) + AIm,R (x).                             (17.1)
One cannot replace the right side by 2n without additionally verifying the time-reversed section
tests.
Proof. The first-variation identity already retained in (3.1) is ∇q F = R(−p0 , pm ), which proves
the criticality assertion. At a normal terminal collision the reflection sends the incoming velocity
to its negative. The ray therefore retraces the preceding actual flight. At each intervening regular
collision the reversed reflection retraces the next preceding flight. At the normal initial collision
it returns to the original outgoing state. Thus every reversed flight is an actual free flight, with
36                                            QIAN QI

no extra or hidden collision, and the orbit closes after 2m collisions. Its reversed displacement is
the negative of the forward displacement. Its length is twice the original length. No assertion
about a primitive period is used.
   For 1 ≤ j < m the reversed state at time 2m − j is ITRj x, while the states at times 0 and m
are fixed by I. In the original event both endpoint section tests equal one. Summing the m
forward tests and then the terminal test and the m − 1 reversed interior tests gives (17.1), since
the initial test in AIm also equals one. The original section is not assumed invariant under I. □

Corollary 17.4 (One-sided unvisited boundaries without a fictitious orbit). Suppose a designated
finite broken path reaches an unvisited-disk tangency or a section cut, all its visited incidences
remain positive, and its endpoint action is critical. The sector formula (17.5), with the original
physical-side selector, continues to hold in the one-sided coordinate neighbourhood. At a singular
state the second expression in (17.3) is not asserted to be a derivative of a defined physical return
map; the first expression is the one-sided action coefficient.
Proof. The stationary equations for the visited interior collision points have positive-definite
interior Hessian. At the stated limiting path they therefore have a smooth local solution as
functions of the two endpoints. The signed optical corner is nonzero because the visited incidences
and flight lengths are positive. This supplies the smooth action and endpoint-coordinate Jacobian
on a full auxiliary neighbourhood. The portion representing actual trajectories is retained as a
Borel subset E; portions intersecting a forbidden disk are never assigned to the physical event.
On the admitted side these functions agree with the actual action and original measure. The
proof of Theorem 17.3 used arbitrary Borel restrictions, so it applies to this subset without
continuing the physical dynamics across its boundary. Simultaneous cuts are included in the
same selector. If visited grazing also occurs, this particular smooth extension is not invoked.
Its treatment remains the inherited visited-grazing trace argument. Nothing in this corollary
equates an area-null boundary point with a zero positive pairing-endpoint trace.                 □

17.1. What the critical calculation does and does not close. Formula (17.3) identifies
an actual return-stability weight in the original roof density; it is not an ansatz for a Markov
model and not a periodic-orbit replacement of the invariant measure. Formula (17.5) also keeps
positive source on section-cut and one-sided unvisited boundaries rather than assigning it a zero
coefficient by area-nullness. The full paired source, its nongrazing comparison pool, and (15.8)
are unchanged. A critical component coefficient need not equal a discontinuity of the entire
density, since other physical components may be present at the same roof.
   For a collision-uniform payment it is still necessary to control the actual weighted sum on
fixed outer neighbourhoods, its clearance and exact-label localization, and the chart remainders.
Choosing a separate collision-dependent neighbourhood merely to separate critical roof values
would not prove that fixed-window statement. Neither (17.6) nor the unscaled sum (17.7) is
used in that invalid manner. The new identities instead give the exact geometric and stability
coefficients that a positive capacity or transfer-operator estimate must control.

              18. Exact two-mark clearance flux on one physical flight
   The preceding clearance–roof analysis treats a selected near miss as a mark, without adding
a collision. Two different unvisited disks can be marked on the same actual flight. Here we
quantify their physical multiplicity exactly, rather than assuming independence or multiplying
by the number of collision words. The result is a second-order collision-space clearance theorem;
it is not an m2 -normalized pointwise roof estimate.
   Throughout let R ∈ [R− , R+ ], R+ = 47/100, and keep δ∗ = 3/200, 0 < δ1 , δ2 ≤ δ∗ , the
triangular lattice Λ, the actual flight time τ ≤ τ+ , and the one-copy physical mark maps XR , ZR
of Theorem B.2. Define the strictly positive physical separation and the finite comparison set

                           Npair = |W∗ |.
The constant Npair is finite by the same finite-horizon assumption already used in Lemma B.1; it
is not the number of admissible words, the number of pairing sheets, or an unproved distortion
constant.
Lemma 18.1 (Distinct same-flight clearances are uniformly transverse). Let z1 ̸= z2 be two

and lower bound in (18.2). The same decomposition gives |z2 − z1 | ≤ τ+ + 2(R+ + δ∗ ), proving
membership in W∗ . The impact derivative follows from dvϑ⊥ /dϑ = −vϑ with b1 fixed. Neither a
grazing endpoint nor an unvisited near miss is treated as an extra collision.                  □

Proof. Apply Theorem 18.2 with a ≡ 1. Drop only the positive mask in the upper bound.
The denominator is at least cpair by the lattice-spacing calculation, and there are exactly
Npair candidate differences and 23 = 8 sign/root choices. Thus the right side is at most
8Npair δ1 δ2 /(4πRcpair ), which is (18.7). The denominator lower bound is pointwise on the entire
parameter rectangle, not only almost everywhere on the physically admitted pairs.               □

               19. Two clearance marks separated by actual reflections
   The same-flight theorem of Section 18 is an inherited result. We now treat marks on different
flights. The intervening reflections, rather than an independence assumption, supply the new
transversality. This section proves finite-itinerary geometric identities on the original physical
billiard. The uniform sum in the next section explicitly uses the retained physical pressure input;
neither section asserts a normalized roof estimate.
19.1. Physical feet and the reflection derivative. Put κ− = 1/R+ and κ+ = 1/R− . All
radii, clearances, collision coordinates and measures are those of the preceding sections. The
following strengthening of the inherited interior-foot bound is useful because it is independent of
every visited incidence.
Lemma 19.1 (A uniform physical foot standoff). For a genuine mark of clearance 0 < u ≤ δ∗ =
3/200 on a physical flight, its perpendicular foot has distance at least
                                                          √

Proof. The interior assertion is already Lemma B.1; it uses that a third center cannot realize
its small clearance at an incident endpoint. If q is either incident contact and z is the marked
center, lattice spacing gives |z − q| ≥ 1 − R. Orthogonal decomposition along the actual flight
gives t2 = |z − q|2 − (R + u)2 for the distance t > 0 between that endpoint and the foot. The
right side is at least (1 − R+ )2 − (R+ + δ∗ )2 = 1827/40000. Finally 1827 > 144 proves a◦ > 3/50.
No extra collision at the foot has been introduced.                                             □

hold for every such trajectory. They require no lower cutoff on any ci . A visited incidence tending

Proof. For a differentiable variation of an oriented line let J denote its transverse displacement
and J ′ its direction variation, measured at the reference point on that line. Sliding the reference
point along the same line adds only the usual free-flight term. Hence travel by distance t sends
(J, J ′ ) to (J + tJ ′ , J ′ ).
   Here is the reflection calculation with its signs. With outward normal n, tangent J n and
curvature κ = 1/R, write v − = −cn + pJ n and v + = cn + pJ n, where J is rotation by π/2.
A contact variation is δq = J n δs. Its transverse components in the incoming and outgoing
direction frames are −cδs and cδs, respectively. Specular reflection gives δϑ+ = 2κδs − δϑ− .
Reverse the transverse frame after each reflection, applying the same sign reversal to J ′ as to J.
The contact displacement then becomes continuous, while
                                                          2κ
                                           J ′+ = J ′− +     J.
                                                           c
Thus the exact linearized reflection in these unfolded transverse frames is Ki . This is a derivative
of the genuine reflection rule, not an auxiliary reflecting wall at a marked disk.
   The first foot is −σ1 (R + u1 )vϑ⊥ . Differentiating it at fixed u1 gives a vector parallel to vϑ ; its
transverse component is zero and its direction variation is one. Consequently the transverse field
begins with (J, J ′ ) = (0, 1). Propagation to the second foot gives (Bh , Dh ). Differentiating the
second signed distance gives the negative of this transverse component, up to the h frame reversals.
For example, at the last contact, differentiation of b2 = (z2 − qh ) · vh⊥ gives −δqh · vh⊥ − b δϑh . Its
magnitude is exactly Bh . Multiplication by the second impact sign has unit magnitude, proving
both identities in (19.3).
   Each factor in (19.2) has determinant one and nonnegative entries. Replacing all Ki by the
identity can only decrease the product entrywise; the resulting upper-right entry is the sum
of the free lengths. This proves (19.4). At the first collision J = a ≥ a◦ . At each subsequent
collision, propagation from its predecessor obeys Ji+1 = Ji + ℓi Ji′+ with Ji′+ ≥ 2κ− Ji . Hence
Ji+1 ≥ (1 + 2κ− τ− )Ji . The final free flight also increases J, which proves (19.5). All assertions
have been proved on regular physical charts; a one-sided limiting matrix with an infinite grazing
kick is not called a finite derivative of a singular physical return.                                   □
19.2. Global multiplicity, including disconnected word domains. A local nonzero Jacobian
does not bound the number of its roots. The following global argument supplies precisely that
missing fact. A tangentially augmented itinerary consists of the first marked center, the ordered
h actual intervening disk centers, the second marked center, and the two signed impacts. The
marked centers may coincide with one another or with centers visited at other times. They are
not the incident centers of their own marked flights.
Theorem 19.3 (One trajectory per tangentially augmented itinerary). Fix h ≥ 1, the augmented
itinerary, R, and u1 , u2 in their original clearance ranges. There is at most one regular physical
broken path between the two feet realizing these data, including its oriented line at each endpoint.
This is a global statement: no connectedness of its admissible word domain is assumed.
Proof. For two candidate configurations Q = (q0 , q1 , . . . , qh , qh+1 ) and Q,
                                                                               b the endpoint positions
lie on the two auxiliary circles of radii R + u1 and R + u2 , and the other positions lie on the
actual radius-R disks. These auxiliary circles only locate the feet; no new collision is counted.
The Cartesian broken length S(Q) = hi=0 |qi+1 − qi | is convex and continuously differentiable at
                                      P

each candidate because every segment has positive length. Gradient monotonicity gives
                                  (∇S(Q) − ∇S(Q))
                                              b · (Q − Q)
                                                       b ≥ 0.                                      (19.6)
44                                                       QIAN QI

                                                                                                       b i |2 .
At an actual interior collision ∇i S = −2ci ni , so its contribution on the left is −R(ci + cbi )|ni − n
At a marked endpoint the velocity is tangent to its auxiliary circle, with its oriented tangent
fixed by the prescribed impact sign. Thus the endpoint gradient is a fixed sign times J ni . Its
difference is orthogonal to the contact difference, and its contribution to (19.6) is zero. Summing
shows that every actual interior contact agrees, since ci , cbi > 0.
    For completeness, agreement of the contacts alone is not used to skip the endpoint argument.
Write the two convex support gaps for S and add them. Their sum is the left side of (19.6),
now zero. Each gap is a sum of nonnegative support gaps of the Euclidean norm for individual
flight vectors. Equality in the norm support inequality, with nonzero reference vector, forces the
two corresponding vectors to be positive multiples of one another. Therefore all corresponding
flight directions agree. At either endpoint its direction and prescribed impact sign determine its
auxiliary-circle normal uniquely, hence the foot itself. This proves global uniqueness. Repeated
disk centers cause no identification of contact variables at different times and do not affect the
argument.                                                                                                  □

Proof. Apply the exact first-mark formula of Theorem B.2; its prefactor is (4πR)−1 and its

single augmented itinerary cannot produce different roots.

each chart use the ordinary change-of-variables theorem first for bounded nonnegative weights.

never used as a bound. The usual singular collision set is null for collision area and hence for

flights and feet; enlarging that set is allowed only for subsequent positive upper bounds.     □

roof-conditioned or new twisted pressure estimate is assumed.

20.3. What this theorem does, and does not, close. The new derivative, global tangential-
end uniqueness and coarea statements are geometric theorems for the actual billiard. The uniform
complete mark-count theorem is a deduction from the already retained unconditioned physical
pressure input, whose independent reconstruction remains outstanding. It is not presented as
a new proof of that input. The complete double-clearance density and the all-time duplicate
payment are stronger than the inherited same-flight assertions, but their measures are still
different from the target-roof essential height.
   More precisely, for the full original exact event define the positive roof measures
                                       ∗                                     ∗
                    m,δ = L# (1Π Nm,δ νR ),
                   µcount                          µone
                                                    m,δ = L# (1Π 1{Nm,δ ≥1} νR ).

The exact identity µcount = µone + µexcess has µexcess ≥ 0 and the bound (20.15). The first-order
one-mark source is not subtracted from the problem. Even in the unconditioned law, (20.11)
and (20.13) retain its positive first-order term S/R. The exact-label source need not have that
same leading coefficient; no conditioning argument here claims otherwise.
   To close the original theorem, a proof must still control the clearance-selected first-order source
and any excess which enters the actual paired source in the m2 L∞ (dt) topology, including critical,
degenerate, one-sided boundary and section-cut contributions, or dominate that complete source
by the original nongrazing capacity. This bulk identity alone does not identify a paired endpoint
trace with a bulk restriction. The low-incidence shallow source must also be paid. The inherited
precise common closure condition remains (15.8); it has not been renamed or declared true.
   At every fixed outer B the clearance widths are fixed first. Neither the O(ε2 ) mass above nor
the exponential single-trajectory lower bound for Bh is multiplied by m2 and asserted to vanish.
The complete physical sum, the radius and exact-label suprema, the roof essential supremum, the
collision limsup and the final outer B limit retain their original meanings and order. Thus the
raw pointwise local limit and four-coordinate inversion target is unchanged and not yet proved.

         21. The angular clearance source as a weighted unstable family
   The proofs in Sections 19–20 are inherited, including their stated pressure premise. We now
give a different summation route. It starts from the actual angular source in the one-mark flux,
rather than taking a supremum over every physical cylinder. The geometric results of this section
use no pressure or growth assumption. The propagation theorem in the next section uses the
explicitly specified homogeneous curve-growth input, not the stronger physical-word pressure
input of Input 11.1.

21.1. A genuine first collision, not a reflection at the mark. Fix 0 < u ≤ δ∗ and

when differentiating. The marked disk itself is never reflected from at the foot.

Lemma 21.2 (A uniform branch count). For each fixed R, u, σ, the angular circle can be cut
at at most 8N∗ points, in addition to an angular coordinate seam, so that on every resulting
interval the first collision center is fixed, the contact coordinate has no seam, and the image in
Theorem 21.1 is injective.
                       q      The number of intervals is at most M∗ = 8N∗ + 1; each has image
length at most 2πR+ 1 + U02 .
Proof. Every first collision center belongs to the stated candidate set: its distance from the
marked center is at most τ+ + 2R+ + δ∗ . For a candidate z, loss or creation of an intersection of
the line with its disk requires
                               z · vϑ⊥ + b = R    or z · vϑ⊥ + b = −R.
Each equation has at most two roots on the angular circle, since z ̸= 0. There are thus at
most 4N∗ tangency cuts. Away from these cuts, positive first-hit times vary continuously. Their
ordering cannot change without two distinct disks having a common contact point, which is
impossible because 2R < 1. No intersection can pass through the foot, which is exterior to every
disk as proved above. Thus the first target is fixed on each interval.
   At each candidate disk choose one contact-coordinate seam, at q = z + R(1, 0). A line in
the source passes through this point only if q · vϑ⊥ = −b. Since |q| ≥ 1 − R+ > b∗ , there are
at most two such roots for each disk. We allow 4N∗ seam cuts in the displayed bound, which
is a harmless overestimate. Coincident roots only reduce the count. On a seam-free interval
ds/dϑ = −a/c < 0, so s is strictly monotone and cannot wind around its contact circle. Its range
has length at most 2πR. This proves injectivity and the length bound. The argument uses no
long collision-word count. Overlapping images from different intervals remain separately labeled
sources; they are not silently identified in the quotient collision space.                    □

22.2. The original roof obstruction has not been replaced. The new norm in (21.8)
describes a singular one-dimensional angular source in collision space. The density in (22.4)
is in second clearance. It is not a density in the original roof, and the complete root bound
in (22.5) has no hidden m−2 factor. The unconditioned first-order mass S/R remains positive.
An exact-label selection can change its leading coefficient; it is not replaced by a probability
conditioned on a mark.
   To reach the raw target, the original ordered shortage limit (15.8) must still be proved. In
particular the first-order marked source must be estimated after the complete original roof
pushforward and physical word sum, including fold, critical, higher-degeneracy, one-sided and
pairing-boundary terms, with the required m2 normalization. The single-mark fixed-clearance
roof bridge in (E.3) remains only a sufficient unproved condition; it is not a premise added to
our main problem. The local fold calculations show why it is safer to integrate clearance before
taking a roof supremum. Low-incidence shallow source and nongrazing capacity likewise remain
in the original shortage. At fixed outer B, multiplying (22.8) by m2 does not prove decay. No
collision-dependent roof window, altered section, exceptional-set subtraction or changed order of
limits is introduced here.

                     23. A grazing-uniform curvature graph transform
   The preceding numbered proof bodies are retained verbatim. We now discharge one of their
explicitly exposed premises: admissibility of the actual angular source for homogeneous curve
growth. This is not a deduction of roofwise pressure from a curvature bound. We prove curvature
invariance here, one-step weighted complexity and distortion in the next section, and only
then apply a precisely identified curve-sum lemma. No event or collision is changed by these
constructions.
   Write t∗ = τ− = 3/50, k− = 100/47, k+ = 20/9, and U = k+ + t−1       ∗ = 170/9. At a fixed

V = dφ/ds ∈ [κ, U ]. Their signed geometric curvature is

one actual regular collision.

Remark 23.3 (What the norm conversion does and does not do). The graph second derivative
on this invariant class is at most B2 = Bcv (1 + U 2 )3/2 . This is a consequence of geometric
curvature invariance, not a claim that the same bound on φ′′ is itself an invariant rectangular
constraint. The class in (H2) of [6] is formulated with geometric curvature; equivalence of these
two boundedness notions is used only for derivative estimates. The optical Jacobi curvature and
the long-record roof Hessian are different quantities and are not controlled by (23.4).

             24. Physical one-step complexity and homogeneous distortion
   We verify the remaining curve-growth hypotheses for precisely the class constructed above.
The argument concerns actual one-flight branches and all their homogeneous pieces. It is not
an estimate obtained by counting long collision words. Constants may depend on the fixed
original geometric family and the chosen finite curvature allowance Bcv , but not on R, incidences,
clearance marks, collision separation or exact-event selectors.
24.1. Uniform finite geometry and a one-sided first-hit lemma. The retained τ+ can
be defined intrinsically as the supremum of lengths of straight free segments in the original
family. For clarity, its finiteness also follows directly from the fixed lattice. A complete line of
irrational direction is dense on R2 /Λ and meets the open radius-R− disk. A line of rational
direction
 √         is parallel
               √       to a primitive lattice vector z, with neighboring
                                                                    √       lattice rows at distance
( 3/2)/|z| ≤ 3/2. Its distance from one such row is at most 3/4 < R− . It therefore meets
open disks on that row. The same argument applies to half-lines. The direction dichotomy
follows, for example, by describing the closure of a line subgroup of a two-dimensional torus: a
proper connected subgroup has an integral annihilator and is a rational circle; otherwise the
closure is the full torus. The positive semiorbit has the same closure by recurrence of translation
on this compact group. These elementary observations use the Euclidean triangular lattice, not
a linear deformation of the physical disks.
   If free segments of unbounded length existed, translate their midpoints into a compact
fundamental cell and take a convergent subsequence of midpoints and directions. Every finite
subsegment of the limiting complete line would avoid every open radius-R− disk, a contradiction.
Thus τ+ < ∞, uniformly also for R ≥ R− . This compactness proof does not assign a sampled
numerical value to τ+ . Put
              Df = τ+ + 2R+ ,       Nf = #{z ∈ Λ : 0 < |z| ≤ Df },         Jf = 2Nf + 1.          (24.1)
The packing bound Nf + 1 ≤ 4(Df + 1/2)2 is fully geometric.
  Choose a preliminary curve length small enough that the relative angle ηz = arg(z − q) − ϑ
varies by at most π/2 for every candidate. This is possible uniformly because |z − q| ≥ 1 − R+
and ϑs = κ + V ≤ k+ + U . On every such graph,
     ηz′ ≤ (1 − R+ )−1 − 2k− < 0,    b′z = −c − tz (κ + V ),   tz = (z − q) · v,   bz = (z − q) · v ⊥ .
                          COLLISION RECORDS AND RAW LOCAL INVERSION                                  57

The positive-foot set is an interval, and bz is strictly decreasing there. Every disk-intersection set
is consequently an interval, with at most two tangency endpoints. The initial point is exterior to
every other disk, so tz > 0 and |bz | < R are exactly the conditions for a positive intersection.
Distinct first-entry times cannot exchange their order without a common point of two disjoint
disks. Cutting at these at most 2Nf tangencies gives at most Jf genuine first-hit branches.
Coordinate seams are glued on the collision circle, not counted as new physical branches. In
a seam-free local lift the image is a graph and φ1 is strictly monotone by Lemma 23.1. Each
first-hit branch thus meets each target homogeneity strip at most once.
Lemma 24.1 (At most one non-grazing limiting branch). Fix k0 ≥ 2, with the strips of
Theorem 21.3. There is a common ℓ0 (k0 ) > 0 such that every allowed curve of length at most
ℓ0 (k0 ) has at most one connected physical image piece in H0 . This assertion counts maximal
physical homogeneous pieces before the length subdivision used in the growth partition.
Proof. We give the local argument also at a limiting initial grazing state. Fix a limiting radius
and outgoing state in the compact closure of the collision cylinder. Consider its oriented straight
ray and the first disk which it intersects strictly. Such a disk exists by the preceding finite
geometry, even if the ray is tangent to one or more earlier disks. The strict first-entry time
and center persist continuously nearby. The finitely many earlier possible contacts in the limit
are tangencies. Any nearby first-hit branch on one of these earlier disks has terminal incidence
tending to zero, hence cannot have its image in H0 in a sufficiently small neighborhood. A later
disk cannot be the next collision when the persistent strict disk is met.
   It remains to check connectivity for the persistent strict disk, rather than infer it just from
uniqueness
      q      of a limiting center. At an earlier tangency bz = ±R the positive foot satisfies
tz ≥ (1 − R+ )2 − R+    2 > 0. Thus b′ is uniformly negative there on all allowed graphs. In a
                                         z
sufficiently small neighborhood this candidate can cross only one of its two tangency levels:
the levels are separated by 2R ≥ 2R− and |b′z | has a uniform finite upper bound on the finite
candidate set. Its intersection condition is therefore one-sided along any short source graph.
Avoiding all earlier disks is an intersection of one-sided intervals, hence an interval. The strict-disk
branch on that interval has monotone φ1 , so restricting it to H0 is still an interval. Incipient
tangencies after the strict contact have no effect on which collision is first.
   These neighborhoods are open in radius and the compact closed collision cylinder. A finite
subcover, and a smaller common curve length, give ℓ0 (k0 ); the bounded cone slopes keep each
sufficiently short graph inside a member of this cover. Equivalently a sequence of shrinking
counterexample curves would converge to one of the limiting states just analyzed. Only this
limiting line is allowed to pass through tangency points for purposes of classification. Every
branch being estimated is an actual regular billiard flight with its actual first collision. No
tangency is erased from a physical record.                                                            □

      25. Admissible physical curve growth and the clearance consequence
  We now separate the mathematical application of a published combinatorial lemma from the
inherited global spectral and roof-pressure inputs. The application below is a theorem of this
revision, not a claim that a successful build has certified the billiard dynamics.

Proof. Fix k0 by (24.3); choose ℓ∗ no larger than the length from Lemma 24.1, the short-curve

   Theorem 23.2 verifies invariance of the homogeneous C 2 curve class in the literal geometric-
curvature sense of (H2) in [6]. It contains the original first-image source graphs; this membership
is now proved, not inferred from their being C 2 . Equation (24.2) gives the common adapted
hyperbolicity and a uniform endpoint metric comparison. Theorem 24.2 verifies (H3.A) and
(H3.B), with the explicit permissible threshold ζ0 = 1/2. Its estimate uses suprema of contraction,
so it is at least as strong as the one-step hypothesis used there. Theorem 24.4 gives the arclength
and invariant-measure parts of (H4) on homogeneous branches. The logarithmic and ratio forms
are converted on the one fixed short length. None of these verifications uses the physical-word
pressure input.
   For completeness, singular-null coverage is checked on these curves. At each forward step there
are only finitely many physical tangency cuts on a short graph, by the positive-foot monotonicity
above. Its regular image has strictly monotone φ1 , so homogeneous boundaries have at most
one preimage per first-hit branch per strip. The additional length cuts are countable. Starting
with a countable collection, iteration thus excludes a countable collection of points for each finite
l. Each regular restriction is injective. No positive arclength part of a graph lies in a tangency
or homogeneous-boundary preimage. This proves the asserted null coverage without treating a
bulk-null set as a zero endpoint trace in any pairing construction.
   We use precisely [6, Lemma 3.2(b), p. 14]: for the stable class and the homogeneous inverse
partition Gl (W ), with long pieces subdivided at the fixed short scale, the sum of sup arclength
contraction Jacobians is at most a constant depending on the common curve, expansion and
distortion constants, uniformly in W , T and l. The published proof specifies (H2)–(H4) as its
requirements; it is not a theorem about all cylinder suprema or roof Fourier transforms. Apply
this statement to WR    s (B ) = IW u (B ) and reverse it by IT I = T −1 . Since I is an arclength

isometry, its Jacobian conversion is exact and gives (25.1). At l = 0 the sum is one. One may
apply the lemma separately to each R: its constant depends only on the common estimates
just proved. A common Banach-space identification across radii, or a perturbation theorem for
variable boundary lengths, is not required for this curve-sum conclusion. Further fixed shortening
of the scale is included in Cg .                                                                   □

The distinction from the original roofwise theorem. Theorem 25.1 is a complete curve-sum
conclusion and Corollary 25.2 sums all regular physical two-mark branches, including arbitrarily
large separation and arbitrarily small positive incidences. They remove a genuine hypothesis
from that part of the proof chain. They do not turn a clearance density into a roof density. The
count mean S/R is still first order, and the original-clock pushforward can have folds, critical
and one-sided boundary contributions. In particular no factor m−2 occurs in (25.2).

limit is not used to tune the curve partition. No result of this revision establishes (25.3), the full
raw pointwise LLT, or its final four-coordinate inversion. The remaining minimal obligations
along this route are the normalized first-order physical-roof source and complete chart/boundary
sum, the incidence-only low-load source or valid comparison capacity, and the stated original
spectral, arithmetic, pairing and normalization interfaces. The new one-flight complexity and
curve-sum proof does not certify any stronger whole-cylinder or roofwise pressure statement still
used elsewhere.

Remark 26.5 (What is, and is not, smoothed). The bounds sum all relevant regular collision words
before any supremum in R, m, a, v with a, v ∈ (−1, 1), with no endpoint-incidence loss. They have
no whole-cylinder pressure premise. They give no mixing rate, product law, lattice local limit,
or density in the original clock Lm . The grazing-uniform bound follows from the last-collision
cancellation (26.18), not from dropping an inverse incidence factor in a terminal-coordinate
coarea estimate. Singular one-sided critical traces require a separate argument below; they do
not follow by declaring a bulk-null set to have zero trace.

                 27. A pressure-free sum of physical critical sectors
   We now use the endpoint kernel on its own physical source, rather than multiply a local
critical estimate by a count of collision words. The conclusion is a complete, collision-uniform
leading-coefficient sum. It removes a genuine pressure dependency of Proposition 17.5; it is not
yet a normalized roof-density theorem. All endpoint coordinates below are the original angles
q = (α0 , αm ).

   Here are the upper bounds, including their signs and units. For one physical flight of length τ ,
differentiate its Euclidean length with respect to the first contact angle. The diagonal second

auxiliary word is still a smooth action with positive visited incidences. The same calculation is
valid for that auxiliary action, restricted to its actual physical side as in Section 17.         □

27.1. One-sided sectors are paid on the physical side. Bulk singular-nullness alone proves
nothing about an endpoint trace. We give the additional argument for the one-sided critical strata
used in the retained local coarea theorem. Consider a finite or countable one-copy family of critical
auxiliary-action germs x. Every germ has all visited incidences positive and normal endpoints.
Its physical-side set Ex retains all unvisited-obstacle inequalities and the original section and
record cuts. In a neighborhood of x, assume these cuts are a finite Boolean combination of C 1
inequalities whose active boundary differentials are nonzero. The limiting tangent set is then
a finite union of angular sectors. Multiple simultaneous boundaries are permitted; no positive
trace is discarded.
   This class includes the original finite-time section/record cuts on a regular auxiliary word.
A section boundary is a coordinate boundary pulled back by a locally invertible finite collision
map, so its boundary differential is nonzero. The exact occupation count is a finite Boolean
combination of these tests. An unvisited tangency is a clearance boundary: the line-coordinate
determinant in Lemma B.2 makes its differential nonzero, and an interior-foot tangency stays
away from the incident contacts. Only finitely many possible centers occur for a finite word by
finite horizon. Strict displacement labels are locally constant on such a word. General Borel
pairing selectors, or strata involving an actual visited grazing collision, are not asserted to have
this tangent sector property; their exact local selectors and outstanding budgets remain in the
manuscript.

Here ux at an unvisited boundary is the auxiliary inverse weight R|e|/∆, not a claimed derivative
of the singular physical map. This conclusion removes the whole-cylinder pressure premise for this

Proof. First take a finite collection F of distinct physical auxiliary germs. A strictly reflected finite
word with positive visited incidences has an invertible endpoint-momentum map: its endpoint
derivative has nonzero determinant, as follows from e ̸= 0 and ∆ > 0. The inverse-function
theorem gives a common small z-disk for the finitely many local inverses. The absolute Jacobian
from (p0 , pm ) to (p0 , α0 ) on germ x is a positive continuous function ux (z) with ux (0) = ux .
   Distinct regular initial points have disjoint sufficiently small source neighborhoods. The same
is true for distinct strict-word unvisited-boundary germs: at a limiting state, continuing straight
past its unvisited tangencies fixes the next strictly met disk uniquely; iterate this observation
through the fixed m strict collisions. Any alternative word using one of the tangencies would
have a visited zero incidence and is outside this class. Thus two such germs at the same initial
state are the same germ; their selectors are united, not counted twice. This is a uniqueness
argument for the auxiliary extension and does not alter a physical collision record. Artificial
partition seams are identified as well. Finite disjointization of actual source restrictions handles
any remaining coordinate overlap.

27.2. Exact gain and the remaining normalized obstruction. Theorems 27.2 and 27.4
prove a complete leading-coefficient bound at every collision count, including genuine one-sided
physical critical sectors. Their source is the full original momentum coordinate disintegration, and
their constants are uniform on the original radius family. This replaces the pressure-dependent
coefficient estimate, not its historical proof body.
   There is no illicit interchange of a local-radius limit with m → ∞. The sector proof first treats
finitely many germs at a fixed R, m, takes the local radius to zero, and exhausts their positive
coefficients. The resulting bound is uniform in R, m because its right-hand√    side is. It does not
give a common neighborhood size or a summable bound for the local O( s) errors. In particular
the critical values of different germs can differ, so the bound at the common momentum origin
cannot be used as a bound at different momentum points selected by a common physical roof
level.
   The full original problem still requires the unchanged limit (25.3), with B-dependent widths
fixed before the collision limsup. Along the present route the minimal additional inputs are:
a complete original-roof chart/error estimate with the required m−2 normalization, including
the first-order clearance source, noncritical folds and actual visited singular strata; payment of
the original incidence-only low-load source or a valid full nongrazing-capacity comparison; and
the stated spectral, arithmetic, pairing and conditional-denominator inputs with their actual
uniform quantifiers. General pairing selectors retain their exact local angular functions unless
their tangent sectors are proved to exist. Neither the nonzero single-mark mass nor a positive
boundary trace is set to zero. The complete raw pointwise local limit and final four-coordinate
inversion are not asserted by the present theorems.

              28. Exact radial coarea and Borel critical-origin bands
   The preceding critical-sector theorem takes a local limit at fixed collision count and requires a
tangent sector for each physical cut. Here we replace the local Taylor calculation, for an explicitly
defined physical class, by an exact nonlinear calculation. This gives a uniform source estimate
at every band width, including arbitrary Borel cuts. It neither identifies different physical roof
levels nor bounds the paired endpoint measure by the collision measure without a comparison
proof.
   Throughout this section the dynamics, the probability νR    ∗ , the section, and the exact event

Πn,k,m,R are those of the original model. We write Φm (x) = (p0 (x), pm (x)) = ζ and D =

diag(−1, 1). The new polar radius r = |ζ| is an auxiliary coordinate, not the physical clock and
not one of the microscopic widths rB . Fix 0 < ρ < 1 and put

28.1. Critical-origin arms with their physical cuts still present. The next class is defined
by an actual continuation property, not by deleting an inconvenient source. A critical-origin radial
arm is a regular physical inverse along a fixed momentum ray, defined for 0 < r < r+ (θ) ≤ ρ,
with no missing collision on that interval. The arm stays on the same strict visited word. Its
endpoint at r = 0 may be a regular critical orbit, an unvisited/section boundary, or a limit with
a visited tangency; no physical derivative is asserted at that endpoint. All physical points with
r > 0 remain genuine regular orbits. Only one copy of each physical initial point may be used.
   We work with measurable one-copy families of these arms and arbitrary Borel selectors on
them. In particular this includes every original pairing sheet whose incoming limit has |∇q F | = 0
and whose regular interval reaches that limit. The original sheet construction and its measurable,
one-copy parametrization remain the explicitly retained input of Section 3; the present theorem
does not purport to reconstruct that historical construction. Its geometric estimate applies to
every family satisfying the definition, not only to finitely many coordinate balls or to smooth
selectors. Other components of the original event, including arms which terminate at positive
momentum radius before reaching zero, are not assigned zero source and remain outside this
particular estimate.

Apply this first with bounded truncations on finitely many disjoint charts. Monotone convergence
